We remark that since Gaussian FM is essentially equivalent to the standard difusion model (see more in Chapter 6), we will not diferentiate between them unless explicitly stated.

Connection to NODEs. FM can be viewed as a simulation-free alternative to NODEs, introduced in Section 5.1.2. While CNFs require solving ODEs during maximum likelihood training, which is computationally intensive, FM bypasses thi by directly regressing a prescribed velocity field through a simple regression loss. The key insight is that when the marginal density path connecting the source and target distributions is fixed, exact simulation during training becomes unnecessary.

5.2.4 (Optional) Underlying Rules

Continuity Equation: Mass Conservation Criterion. Similar to the PF-ODE and Fokker–Planck analysis in Section 5.2.1, we now present a criterion for verifying whether the density path induced by an ODE flow aligns with a prescribed path $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$

5.2. Flow Matching Framework

Consider the ODE describing the flow of particles under a time-dependent velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ :

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d} \mathbf {x} (t)}{\mathrm{d} t} = \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} (t)). $$

As in Equation (5.2.4), this ODE defines a flow map $\Psi _ { s \to t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } )$ for any $s , t \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ which in particular transports an initial point $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ at time 0 to its state at time t. The induced distribution at time t is given by the pushforward

$$ p _ {t} ^ {\mathrm{fwd}} (\mathbf {x}) = \int \delta \left(\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {x} _ {0})\right) p _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {x} _ {0} =: \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} \# p _ {\mathrm{src}},\tag{5.2.12} $$

so that $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ) \sim p _ { t } ^ { \mathrm { f w d } }$ whenever $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ . Similarly, one can transport backward from $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ to $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ via $\Psi _ { 1 \to 0 } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } )$

Suppose we are given a prescribed density path $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ , and we construct a velocity field $\{ \mathbf { v } _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ to define a particle flow. This naturally raises the question:

Question 5.2.1

Under what conditions does the flow-induced density $p _ { t } ^ { \mathrm { f w d } }$ exactly match the target density $p _ { t }$ for all $t \in [ 0 , 1 ] ?$

Once the two density evolutions align, we can leverage the ODE flow to flexibly transport samples between $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ by solving the ODE.

As in Equation (5.2.5), a principled way to verify this alignment is via the continuity equation, which captures the conservation of mass in time-evolving densities:

Theorem 5.2.2: Mass Conservation Criterion

The flow-induced density $p _ { t } ^ { \mathrm { f w d } }$ equals the prescribed path $p _ { t }$ for all $t \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ i.e.,

$$ p _ {t} ^ {\mathrm{fwd}} = p _ {t}, \quad \text { for all } t \in [ 0, 1 ], $$

if and only if the pair $( p _ { t } , \mathbf { v } _ { t } )$ satisfies the continuity equation:

$$ \partial_ {t} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) + \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \cdot (p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x})) = 0, $$

for all $t \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ and x.

Proof for Theorem.

A conceptual derivation is provided in Section D.3.2, while a more rigorous treatment can be found in (Villani et al., 2008) (see “Mass Conservation Formula”). ■

From Conditional to Marginal Paths. As seen in Section 5.2.2, we begin by defining a conditional probability path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and a corresponding conditiona velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . We then construct the marginal velocity field via:

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \int \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \frac {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z})}{p _ {t} (\mathbf {x})} \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}, $$

as in Equation (5.2.10). However, we still need to ensure that the resulting marginal velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ induces an ODE flow whose density path aligns with the prescribed $p _ { t }$ Fortunately, this verification can be done entirely at the conditional level: if each conditional velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ induces the conditional density path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ , then the resulting marginal velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ also induces the correct marginal path. Formally, this is stated as follows:

Proposition 5.2.3: Marginal VF Generates Given Marginal Density

If the conditional velocity fields $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ induce conditional density paths that match $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ (starting from $p _ { 0 } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } ) )$ then the marginal velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot )$ defined in Equation (5.2.10) induces a marginal density path that aligns with $p _ { t } ( \cdot )$ , starting from $p _ { 0 } ( \cdot )$

Proof for Proposition.

This result follows by verifying that the pair $( p _ { t } , \mathbf { v } _ { t } )$ satisfies the Continuity Equation. We present the argument in a converse manner to provide intuition for why the marginalized velocity field takes the form in Equation (5.2.10). Since the conditional velocity fields $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ induce density paths matching the conditional densities $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ for $\mathbf { z } \sim \pi$ , the continuity equation holds for each conditional pair:

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) = - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \cdot \left(\mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z})\right).\tag{5.2.13} $$

We aim to find a velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot )$ whose induced densities align with the marginal density $p _ { t }$ , i.e., satisfy

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \cdot \big (\mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) \big).\tag{5.2.14} $$

Starting from the definition of $p _ { t }$ in Equation (5.2.7),

$$ \begin{array}{r l} & {\frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \int \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}} \\ & {\qquad = - \int \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \cdot \Big (\mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \Big) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}} \\ & {\qquad = - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \cdot \Big (\int \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z} \Big),} \end{array} $$

where the second equality follows by applying Equation (5.2.13). Comparing this with the right-hand side of Equation (5.2.14) shows that, up to a divergence-free

5.2. Flow Matching Framework

term,

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \int \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}. $$

Therefore, we can define

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) := \int \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) \frac {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z})}{p _ {t} (\mathbf {x})} \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}, $$

which is precisely the form in Equation (5.2.10). The proof of this theorem essentially follows the reverse of this argument. ■

This connection allows us to reduce the construction of the potentially in tractable marginal velocity field to defining simpler conditional fields $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ , which are easier to work with by construction.

5.3 Constructing Probability Paths and Velocities Between Distributions

The essence of flow matching lies in the gradual transformation of a source distri bution into a target. To direct this transformation, two key elements are needed: the probability path $p _ { t }$ , which provides a snapshot of the evolving distribution at each time t, and the velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ , which describes how individual particles move along the path. These two objects are not independent; they are linked through the continuity equation, which ensures that particle dynamics are consistent with the evolution of the distribution. Thus, the learning task reduces to finding a velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ that faithfully drives the process. The dificulty, however, is that for general and complex distributions, the true marginal velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t }$ is unknown, leaving us with an intractable target that cannot be accessed directly.

The core idea of Conditional Flow Matching is to address the intractability of the true marginal velocity by constructing an artificial but tractable process. To do this, we introduce a conditioning variable z and design either a conditional velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } )$ and/or a conditional path $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } )$ , which are deliberately chosen to be simple.

Because these conditional objects are known in closed form, they serve as surrogate targets that the model can regress against. This leads to a valid training loss $\mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { C F M } }$ , provided two practical requirements are met: (i) we can sample eficiently from $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ , and (ii) the corresponding velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ admits a closed-form expression.

How should we design a well behaved conditional process? For inspiration, we turn to the one case that is fully understood: the Gaussian to Gaussian bridge (Section 5.3.1). This example highlights two natural design strategies: adopt a Gaussian probability path at each time t, or prescribe an afine velocity field, both of which are analytically tractable.

Guided by this insight, we extend to general endpoint distributions with two complementary views (see also Section B.1.2) for constructing conditional paths and velocities:

■ Conditional Probability Path First (Eulerian View). It begins with a conditional probability path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and derives the corresponding conditional velocity field.

■ Conditional Flow First (Lagrangian View). It starts from a conditional flow $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ , typically afine, and derives the conditional velocity field by diferentiating with respect to time along trajectories.

In Section 5.3.2, we detail the first approach, which shows its close analogy to difusion model construction discussed in Section 5.2.1, while in Section 5.3.3 we present the second. Together, these perspectives provide a practical framework for defining $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } )$ and $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } )$ , enabling simulation-free training and the construction of flows between arbitrary source and target distributions.

5.3.1 A Key Special Case: Marginal $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ and Velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ in the Gaussianto-Gaussian Bridge

We begin with the Gaussian–endpoint case, where we can compute the marginal density $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ and velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ analytically. This serves as a template for the general construction of the conditional density $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } _ { t } )$ and velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } _ { t } )$

When the source and target distributions, $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ <sub>t</sub>, are both Gaussian, the velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot )$ admits a closed-form expression. We consider the interpolated marginal density path:

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \mathcal {N} \left(\mathbf {x} _ {t}; \pmb {\mu} (t), \sigma^ {2} (t) \mathbf {I}\right),\tag{5.3.1} $$

with time-varying mean $\pmb { \mu } ( t )$ and variance $\sigma ^ { 2 } ( t ) > 0$ . The two endpoints are given by

$$ p _ {\mathrm{src}} = p _ {0} = \mathcal {N} (\mathbf {x}; \boldsymbol {\mu} (0), \sigma^ {2} (0) \mathbf {I}), \quad p _ {\mathrm{tgt}} = p _ {1} = \mathcal {N} (\mathbf {x}; \boldsymbol {\mu} (1), \sigma^ {2} (1) \mathbf {I}), $$

so that the path $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ connects these distributions.

With the given path $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ , there are indeed many velocity fields that induce an ODE flow $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \mathbf { x } )$ such that $\mathbf { x } \sim p _ { 0 }$ implies $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \mathbf { x } ) \sim p _ { t }$ . For this Gaussian path, a particularly simple realization is given $\mathrm { b y } ^ { 7 }$

$$ \Psi_ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {x}) := \boldsymbol {\mu} (t) + \sigma (t) \left(\frac {\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (0)}{\sigma (0)}\right).\tag{5.3.2} $$

For the defined Gaussian path $p _ { t }$ (Gaussian for all $t )$ , the velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot )$ inducing the ODE flow in Equation (5.3.2) is uniquely and analytically characterized as follows (Lipman et $a l .$ , 2022):

Proposition 5.3.1: Closed-Form Velocity Field for Gaussian Density Path

Let $p _ { t }$ be the Gaussian path in Equation (5.3.1). Then the velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot )$ that generates the ODE flow Equation (5.3.2) is unique for the defined $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ and has the closed-form expression:

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \frac {\sigma^ {\prime} (t)}{\sigma (t)} \left(\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (t)\right) + \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {\prime} (t). $$

Proof for Proposition.

Consider the ODE with initial condition y:

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {y}) = \mathbf {v} _ {t} \big (\boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {y}) \big). $$

Since $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ is invertible $( \mathrm { a s ~ } \sigma ( t ) > 0 )$ , we may set $\mathbf { x } = \Psi _ { 0 t } ( \mathbf { y } )$ and ${ \bf y } =$ $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { x } ) = \Psi _ { t \to 0 } ( \mathbf { x } )$ to obtain

$$ \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} ^ {\prime} \big (\boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {x}) \big) = \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}). $$

Diferentiating Equation (5.3.2) with respect to t gives

$$ \Psi_ {0 \rightarrow t} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {x}) = \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {\prime} (t) + \sigma^ {\prime} (t) \left(\frac {\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (0)}{\sigma (0)}\right). $$

Solving for $\mathbf { y } = \Psi _ { 0 \to t } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { x } )$ yields

$$ \mathbf {y} = \boldsymbol {\mu} (0) + \sigma (0) \left(\frac {\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (t)}{\sigma (t)}\right). $$

Substituting this into $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ^ { \prime } ( \mathbf { x } )$ gives

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \frac {\sigma^ {\prime} (t)}{\sigma (t)} \left(\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (t)\right) + \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {\prime} (t), $$

as claimed.

We note that for a fixed flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ (flow-first view), the velocity is uniquely determined by

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} = \partial_ {t} \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} \circ \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} ^ {- 1}. $$

Under this construction, the pair $( p _ { t } , \mathbf { v } _ { t } )$ automatically satisfies the continuity equation. By contrast, for a given density path $t \mapsto p _ { t }$ without fixing $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ (probability-path-first view), the velocity field is not unique.

This distinction precisely characterizes the diference between the flow-first and probability-path-first perspectives.

This closed-form characterization remains valid when conditioning on a latent variable $\mathbf { z } .$ In the following, we extend this insight to construct a conditional Gaussian path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and derive the corresponding conditional velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ for the general marginal setting.

5.3.2 Conditional Probability-Path-First Construction of $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$

We aim to construct a conditional density path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ first and then derive its corresponding conditional velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ (via Proposition 5.3.1), under conditioning with respect to $\pi ( \mathbf { z } )$ . Depending on how z is chosen, there are two natural scenarios: (i) two-sided conditioning with ${ \bf z } = ( { \bf x } _ { 0 } , { \bf x } _ { 1 } )$ , or (ii) one-sided conditioning with $\mathbf { z } = \mathbf { x } _ { 0 }$ or $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ . In either case, the construction must match the

https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-28/f07f65a6-cc54-4879-9a05-fbe8b1db7b7e/ecb2eb4d26067cfa280f71048da9cdca6ffe81da52b2bb88ef9f0aa946c063ee.jpg" alt="image" loading="lazy">

Figure 5.8: Illustrations of two common types of conditioning probability paths. It includes: (i) two-sided, conditioned on $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ with general endpoint distributions; (ii) one-sided, conditioned at either $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ or $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$

Source: Created by the authors.

boundary distributions:

$$ p _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) = \int p _ {0} (\mathbf {x} _ {0} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}, \quad p _ {\mathrm{tgt}} (\mathbf {x} _ {1}) = \int p _ {1} (\mathbf {x} _ {1} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}. $$

Since verifying these constraints is straightforward once a concrete construction is specified, we do not emphasize the verification step here.

I. Two-Sided ${ \bf z } = ( { \bf x _ { 0 } } , { \bf x _ { 1 } } ) -$ “Beam-Like” Path.

Choice of $\pi ( \mathbf { z } )$ . Consider general distributions $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ over $\mathbb { R } ^ { D }$ . Let ${ \bf z } = ( { \bf x } _ { 0 } , { \bf x } _ { 1 } )$ with $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ independently, i.e.,

$$ \pi (\mathbf {z}) = p _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) p _ {\mathrm{tgt}} (\mathbf {x} _ {1}). $$

Choice of Conditional Path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . Define the conditional path by linear interpolation with fixed variance $\sigma > 0$

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {z} = (\mathbf {x} _ {0}, \mathbf {x} _ {1})) = \mathcal {N} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}; a _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}, \sigma^ {2} \mathbf {I}), $$

where $a _ { t }$ and $b _ { t }$ are time-dependent functions satisfying $a _ { 0 } = 1 , b _ { 0 } = 0$ and $a _ { 1 } = 0 , b _ { 1 } = 1$ . A choice suggested by (Lipman et al., 2022; Liu, 2022) is $a _ { t } = 1 - t _ { : }$ $b _ { t } = t$ . In the deterministic case $\sigma = 0$ , we obtain

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {z}) = \delta \big (\mathbf {x} _ {t} - [ a _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1} ] \big), $$

which describes a deterministic interpolating path from $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } }$ to $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ .

Derived Conditional Velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . By Proposition 5.3.1, the conditional velocity is

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) = a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1}. $$

CFM Loss. When $\sigma = 0$ so that ${ \bf x } _ { t } = a _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 1 }$ , the CFM loss reduces to

$$ \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{CFM}} = \mathbb {E} _ {t, \mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p _ {\mathrm{src}}, \mathbf {x} _ {1} \sim p _ {\mathrm{tgt}}} \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - (a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1}) \right\| ^ {2}. $$

From Equations (5.2.10) and (5.2.11), the optimal velocity field is

$$ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\prime} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} \right] = \mathbb {E} \left[ a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} \right]. $$

Here, the expectation is taken over $p ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } | \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ , the conditional distribution over source-target pairs $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ that could have produced the observed interpolation ${ \bf x } _ { t } = a _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 1 }$ at time t.

II. One-Sided $\bf { z } = \bf { x _ { 0 } }$ or ${ \bf x _ { 1 } } \ -$ “Spotlight-Like” Path. We illustrate the conditional probability–path–first construction in the one-sided setting, considering the standard generative setup with $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } = \mathcal { N } ( \mathbf { 0 } , \mathbf { I } )$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } } = p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ . Crucially, this Gaussian source is not an additional assumption but a direct consequence of the conditional path defined below. A more general treatment of arbitrary endpoint will be given in Section 5.3.3.

Choice of $\pi ( \mathbf { z } )$ . We take $\mathbf { z } = \mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ with $\pi ( \mathbf { z } ) = p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } )$ (the case ${ \bf z } = { \bf x } _ { 0 } \sim$ $p _ { \mathrm { p r i o r } }$ follows analogously).

Choice of Conditional Path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . For fixed $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ , define

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {z} = \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = \mathcal {N} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}; b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}, a _ {t} ^ {2} \mathbf {I}), $$

with $a _ { 0 } = 1 , b _ { 0 } = 0 , a _ { 1 } = 0 , b _ { 1 } = 1$ (usually interpreted as the limit). At the boundaries,

$$ p _ {0} (\cdot | \mathbf {z} = \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = \mathcal {N} (\cdot ; \mathbf {0}, \mathbf {I}), \qquad p _ {1} (\cdot | \mathbf {z} = \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = \delta (\cdot - \mathbf {x} _ {1}). $$

Marginalizing over $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ yields $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ with $p _ { 0 } = \mathcal { N } ( \mathbf { 0 } , \mathbf { I } )$ (independent of $p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } } )$ and $p _ { 1 } = p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$

Derived Conditional Velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . For $t \in ( 0 , 1 )$ with $b _ { t } > 0$ , applying Proposition 5.3.1 to the conditional Gaussian path gives

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} + \frac {a _ {t} ^ {\prime}}{a _ {t}} (\mathbf {x} - b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}). $$

One-Sided CFM Objective. With $t \sim \mathcal { U } ( 0 , 1 )$ (or any fixed sampling distribution) and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ , the CFM loss becomes

$$ \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{CFM}} = \mathbb {E} _ {t, \mathbf {x} _ {1}} \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {t} \sim p _ {t} (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {1})} \Big \| \mathbf {v} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \Big [ b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} + \frac {a _ {t} ^ {\prime}}{a _ {t}} \big (\mathbf {x} _ {t} - b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1} \big) \Big ] \Big \| _ {2} ^ {2}.\tag{5.3.3} $$

By MSE optimality, the unique minimizer is the marginal velocity field

$$ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x}, t) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {x} _ {1}) | d l e | \mathbf {x} _ {t} = \mathbf {x} \right] = \mathbb {E} \left[ a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} | d l e | \mathbf {x} _ {t} = \mathbf {x} \right]. $$

Equivalence to Two-Sided Target. For paired samples $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ with $\mathbf { x } _ { t } =$ $a _ { t } \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } \mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ ,

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} + \frac {a _ {t} ^ {\prime}}{a _ {t}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} - b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1}. $$

Thus the one-sided loss regresses to the conditional expectation of the two-sided target given $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ :

$$ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x}, t) = \mathbb {E} \big [ a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} = \mathbf {x} \big ], $$

so the one-sided and two-sided CFM objectives share the same minimizer.

Gaussian FM = Difusion Model. We use the FM convention where $t = 0$ denotes the source/prior and $t = 1$ denotes the target/data:

$$ p _ {\mathrm{src}} = p _ {\mathrm{prior}}, \qquad p _ {\mathrm{tgt}} = p _ {\mathrm{data}}. $$

By contrast, difusion models typically index time from data to noise $( \mathrm { i . e . , } t = 0$ is data and $t = 1$ is prior). Here, we adopt the FM convention indexing in the main discussion. For comparison, however, the VE and VP entries in Table 5.2 are summarized in their usual difusion-time indexing, where $t = 0$ corresponds to data and $t = 1$ to prior/noise. If further $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } = \mathcal { N } ( \mathbf { 0 } , \mathbf { I } )$ , then for fixed condition $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ , the conditional path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } )$ is naturally chosen to be Gaussian, while the target distribution $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ itself need not be Gaussian. Some literature usually refer to this setting as Gaussian FM.

Choosing $a _ { t } = 1 - t$ and $b _ { t } = t$ (equivalently, $\alpha _ { t } = t$ and $\sigma _ { t } = 1 - t$ under the relabeling $a _ { t } : = \sigma _ { t } , b _ { t } : = \alpha _ { t }$ in difusion model) recovers the familiar $\mathrm { F M / R F }$ schedule (Lipman et al., 2022; Liu, 2022).

Table 5.2: Summary of diferent interpolants written as ${ \bf x } _ { t } = a _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 1 }$ , where $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } = p _ { \mathrm { p r i o r } }$ and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } } = p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ . The $\mathrm { F M / R F }$ and trigonometric columns use the FM convention $( t = 0$ source, $t = 1$ target). For comparison, the VE/VP columns retain their usual difusion-time indexing (t = 0 data, t = 1 prior/noise), with coeficient names relabeled as $a _ { t } : = \sigma _ { t }$ and $b _ { t } : = \alpha _ { t }$

<table><tr><td></td><td>VE</td><td>VP</td><td>FM/RF</td><td>Trig. (Albergo et al., 2023)</td></tr><tr><td><eq>a_t</eq> (prior coeff.)</td><td><eq>a_t</eq></td><td><eq>\sqrt{1 - b_t^2}</eq></td><td><eq>1 - t</eq></td><td><eq>\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)</eq></td></tr><tr><td><eq>b_t</eq> (data coeff.)</td><td>1</td><td><eq>b_t</eq></td><td><eq>t</eq></td><td><eq>\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)</eq></td></tr><tr><td><eq>a_0</eq></td><td>0</td><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td><eq>b_0</eq></td><td>1</td><td>1</td><td>0</td><td>0</td></tr><tr><td><eq>a_1</eq></td><td><eq>a_1</eq></td><td>1</td><td>0</td><td>0</td></tr><tr><td><eq>b_1</eq></td><td>1</td><td>0</td><td>1</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td><eq>p_{\text{prior}}</eq></td><td><eq>\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, a_1^2\mathbf{I})</eq></td><td><eq>\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I})</eq></td><td><eq>\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I})</eq></td><td><eq>\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I})</eq></td></tr></table>

In the Gaussian FM setting, both the beam-like and spotlight-like conditional paths lead to training objectives that are similar to the standard difusion losses. As we will elaborate in Chapter 6, Gaussian FM can in fact be equivalently interpreted as a difusion model trained to predict the velocity, under the linear schedule $a _ { t } = 1 - t$ and $b _ { t } = t$ . This perspective highlights that flow matching and difusion are not fundamentally diferent, but rather two equivalent formulations that can be transformed into one another. The Gaussian FM objective is particularly appealing in practice: its loss function $\left( \mathbb { E } _ { t , \mathbf { x } _ { t } } \left[ \left. \mathbf { v } _ { \phi } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } , t ) - ( \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } - \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ) \right. _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] \right)$ ) is simple, and it has been shown to achieve competitive performance at scale (Esser et al., 2024).

5.3.3 Conditional Flow-First Construction of $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$

We treat the general case where the endpoints $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } \ ( \mathrm { a t } \ t = 0 )$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } } \ ( \mathrm { a t } \ t = 1 )$ are arbitrary. Our goal is to design, directly in trajectory space, a conditional flow that transports samples from $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ to $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ and yields a closed-form $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { z } )$ usable as a regression target.

Motivation. Instead of first designing conditional density path, we may directly specify a conditional flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } \big ( \cdot ; \mathbf { z } \big )$ that moves samples along trajectories. This has two practical advantages: (i) it immediately yields a regression target for training via a time derivative along trajectories; (ii) on geometry-structured spaces (Riemannian manifolds, Lie groups, or constrained submanifolds), it is often natural to construct the conditional flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ directly from the geometry (e.g., geodesics, exponential maps, or premetrics) (Lipman et al., 2024) which yields analytic, simulation-free target velocities for training.

Conditional Afine Flow (Link to Proposition 5.3.1). We fix a conditioning variable $\mathbf { z } \sim \pi \ ( \mathrm { e . g . , } \ \mathbf { z } = \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ in one-sided “spotlight’’ training) and push forward $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ through the time-varying conditional afine flow

$$ \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}; \mathbf {z}) := \boldsymbol {\mu} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) + \mathbf {A} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) \mathbf {x} _ {0}, \quad t \in [ 0, 1 ], $$

where $\pmb { \mu } _ { t } ( \mathbf { z } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { D }$ and $\mathbf { A } _ { t } ( \mathbf { z } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { D \times D }$ is invertible for $t \in ( 0 , 1 )$ . The boundary $\mathbf { A } _ { 0 } ( \mathbf { z } ) = \mathbf { I }$ 2 $\pmb { \mu } _ { 0 } ( \mathbf { z } ) = \mathbf { 0 }$ recovers $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ at $t = 0$ . It is standard to interpret boundary when $t 1$ as a limit (the terminal map may concentrate mass on a lowerdimensional set or a point)<sup>8</sup>.

Induced Conditional Path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . The construction defines

$$ p _ {t} (\cdot | \mathbf {z}) = \left(\Psi_ {0 \rightarrow t} (\cdot ; \mathbf {z})\right) _ {\#} p _ {\mathrm{src}}, \quad p _ {t} (\cdot) = \int p _ {t} (\cdot | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {z}. $$

What ultimately matters in $\mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { C F M } }$ is how to sample from it: first draw $\mathbf { z } \sim \pi$ , then draw $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ , and finally set

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = \pmb {\mu} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) + \mathbf {A} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) \mathbf {x} _ {0}. $$

We remark that when $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ is afine in $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } }$ , then $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ is Gaussian if and only if $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ is Gaussian. In particular, for arbitrary (non-Gaussian) $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ , an afine flow yields a generally non-Gaussian $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$

Derived Conditional Velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ . The conditional velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ is obtained by t-diferentiating the conditional flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t }$ . Following the derivation in Proposition 5.3.1, consider the conditional ODE defined by the flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \mathbf { y } ; \mathbf { z } )$ with initial condition y, where the goal is to identify the corresponding conditional velocity field $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {y}; \mathbf {z}) = \mathbf {v} _ {t} \Bigl (\underbrace {\boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {y} ; \mathbf {z})} _ {\mathbf {x}} \Big | \mathbf {z} \Bigr). $$

Since $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \cdot ; \mathbf { z } )$ is invertible for $t \in ( 0 , 1 )$ , we may express y in terms of the current state $\mathbf { x } : = \Psi _ { 0 t } ( \mathbf { y } ; \mathbf { z } )$ as $\mathbf { y } = \boldsymbol { \Psi } _ { 0 t } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathbf { x } ; \mathbf { z } ) = \boldsymbol { \Psi } _ { t 0 } ( \mathbf { x } ; \mathbf { z } )$ . Substituting this into the ODE yields the following construction of the conditional velocity field:

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) := \frac {\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} \big (\boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {t \rightarrow 0} (\mathbf {x}; \mathbf {z}); \mathbf {z} \big), $$

which makes explicit that the derivative must be taken along the trajectory that reaches the spatial point x at time t.

Since ${ \bf x } _ { t } = \pmb { \mu } _ { t } ( { \bf z } ) + { \bf A } _ { t } ( { \bf z } ) { \bf x } _ { 0 }$ and ${ \bf A } _ { t } ( { \bf z } )$ is invertible on $( 0 , 1 )$ , we have $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } =$ ${ \bf A } _ { t } ( { \bf z } ) ^ { - 1 } ( { \bf x } - \mu _ { t } ( { \bf z } ) )$ , giving

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {z}) = \boldsymbol {\mu} _ {t} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {z}) + \mathbf {A} _ {t} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {z}) \mathbf {A} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) ^ {- 1} \bigl (\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} _ {t} (\mathbf {z}) \bigr). $$

One-Sided Conditioning $( \mathbf { z } = \mathbf { x _ { 1 } } )$ Choosing $\pmb { \mu } _ { t } ( \mathbf { z } ) = b _ { t } \mathbf { z }$ and $\mathbf { A } _ { t } ( \mathbf { z } ) = a _ { t } \mathbf { I }$ with $a _ { 0 } = 1 , a _ { 1 } = 0$ and $b _ { 0 } = 0 , b _ { 1 } = 1$ (with $a _ { t } > 0$ for $t \in ( 0 , 1 ) )$ yields

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = a _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}, \qquad \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} | \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1} + \frac {a _ {t} ^ {\prime}}{a _ {t}} (\mathbf {x} - b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}). $$

On paired samples $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ (with ${ \mathbf x } _ { t } = a _ { t } { \mathbf x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } { \mathbf x } _ { 1 } )$ , this simplifies to the usua CFM target:

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1}. $$

Two-Sided Conditioning $\bf ( z = ( x _ { 0 } , x _ { 1 } ) )$ . The same template with $\mu _ { t } ( { \bf x } _ { 0 } , { \bf x } _ { 1 } ) =$ $b _ { t } \mathbf { x } _ { 1 }$ and ${ \bf A } _ { t } ( { \bf x } _ { 0 } , { \bf x } _ { 1 } ) = a _ { t } { \bf I }$ makes the conditional path deterministic:

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = a _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}, \qquad p _ {t} (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {0}, \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = \delta \left(\cdot - (a _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {1})\right), $$

and the conditional velocity is

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}, \mathbf {x} _ {1}) = a _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} + b _ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {1}, $$

i.e., the standard two-sided CFM target.

Unconditional Gaussian Path as a Special Case. If $\pmb { \mu } _ { t }$ is independent of z (denoted $\pmb { \mu } ( t ) )$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf A _ { t } = \frac { \sigma ( t ) } { \sigma ( 0 ) } \mathbf I } \end{array}$ , then

$$ \boldsymbol {\Psi} _ {0 \rightarrow t} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) = \boldsymbol {\mu} (t) + \sigma (t) \frac {\mathbf {x} _ {0} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (0)}{\sigma (0)}, \quad \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x}) = \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {\prime} (t) + \frac {\sigma^ {\prime} (t)}{\sigma (t)} (\mathbf {x} - \boldsymbol {\mu} (t)), $$

which recovers the Gaussian density path and the closed-form velocity in Proposi tion 5.3.1.

5.3.4 Probability-Path-First vs. Flow-First Construction

Both constructions aim to connect a source distribution $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and a target distribu tion $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ through conditional dynamics. The probability-path-first (Eulerian) view begins by positing a conditional density path $p _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ , often chosen from Gaussian or afine families so that the associated velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ can be solved analytically. The flow-first (Lagrangian) view instead specifies a conditional flow map $\Psi _ { 0 \to t } ( \cdot | \mathbf { z } )$ and obtains the velocity directly by diferentiation along particle trajectories. While both yield equivalent transport under regularity, they difer in identifiability, ease of computation, and how endpoint constraints are enforced. The following table summarizes these contrasts. The takeaway: path-first is natural when conditional paths admit closed-form velocities; flow-first is natural when you have strong structural priors on trajectories.

<table><tr><td>Axis</td><td>Conditional Probability-Path-First</td><td>Conditional Flow-First</td></tr><tr><td>Given</td><td>Conditional density path <eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>.</td><td>Conditional flow map <eq>\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> (trajectories, for each fixed <eq>\mathbf{z}</eq>).</td></tr><tr><td>Get Velocity</td><td>For each <eq>\mathbf{z}</eq>, find <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> s.t.<eq>\partial_t p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) + \nabla \cdot (p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) \mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})) = 0;</eq>Non-unique: if <eq>\nabla \cdot (p_t \mathbf{w}_t) = 0</eq> then <eq>\mathbf{v}_t + \mathbf{w}_t</eq> yields the same <eq>p_t</eq>.</td><td>Along paths (for each <eq>\mathbf{z}</eq>):<eq>\mathbf{v}_t (\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})|\mathbf{z}) = \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t} \Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>.When <eq>\Psi_{0\to t}</eq> is invertible, one can solve<eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{z}) = \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t} \Psi_{0\to t}(\Psi_{0\to t}^{-1}(\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{z})</eq>.</td></tr><tr><td>Closed Form of <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq></td><td>Convenient when <eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> is Gaussian / exponential-family; otherwise obtaining <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> is nontrivial.</td><td>Convenient when <eq>\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> has structure (affine/low-rank); avoids density evaluation.</td></tr><tr><td>Uniqueness of <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq></td><td>For each <eq>\mathbf{z}</eq>, <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> is underdetermined unless a selection rule (e.g., potential flow / min. kinetic energy) is imposed.</td><td>Given <eq>\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>, both <eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) = (\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z}))_\# p_0</eq> and <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> are determined; non-invertible maps still define <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> along trajectories, while invertible ones make it unique.</td></tr><tr><td>Realizability</td><td>Must verify the constructed <eq>\mathbf{v}_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> solving the continuity equation on the intended support.</td><td>Holds by construction:<eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) = (\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z}))_\# p_0(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>.</td></tr><tr><td>Match (<eq>p_{src}</eq>, <eq>p_{tgt}</eq>)</td><td>Mix conditionals:<eq>p_{src} = \int p_0(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) \pi(\mathbf{z}) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{z},</eq><eq>p_{tgt} = \int p_1(\cdot|\mathbf{z}) \pi(\mathbf{z}) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}.</eq>Under Gaussian-affine conditional paths with <eq>\mathbf{z}</eq>-independent coefficients, <eq>p_{src}</eq> can be forced to be Gaussian. For a general fixed endpoint <eq>p_{src}</eq> (possibly non-Gaussian), the choice of <eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq> does not generally pin <eq>p_{src}</eq>.</td><td>Set <eq>\Psi_{0\to 0} = \text{Id}</eq> and choose boundary condition to hit any <eq>p_{tgt}</eq>.</td></tr><tr><td>Preferred Scenarios</td><td>Diffusion-style constructions; analytic targets via conditional Gaussians <eq>p_t(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>.</td><td>Strong structural priors via maps <eq>\Psi_{0\to t}(\cdot|\mathbf{z})</eq>;easy boundary control; accommodates singular/low-dimensional endpoints; natural for map-based regularization/transport costs.</td></tr></table>

5.3.5 On the Misinterpreted “Straightness” of the Canonical Afine Path

It is worth emphasizing that some prior works (Liu, 2022; Lipman et al., 2022) suggest that adopting the canonical afine flow, $a _ { t } = 1 - t$ and $b _ { t } = t .$ , yields “straight-line” ODE trajectories enabling faster sampling. However, this claim does not hold in general.

The key point is that one must distinguish two diferent notions of straightness. Under the canonical afine interpolation

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = (1 - t) \mathbf {x} _ {0} + t \mathbf {x} _ {1}, $$

each conditional path is indeed a straight-line interpolation in time once a particular pair $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ is fixed.

However, the actual sampling dynamics are governed not by a single fixed pair $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ , but by the marginal velocity field

$$ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x}, t) = \mathbb {E} [ \mathbf {x} _ {1} - \mathbf {x} _ {0} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} = \mathbf {x} ]. $$

This is a conditional average over all pairs $\left( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } , \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \right)$ that could have produced the same intermediate point $\mathbf { \Psi } ( \mathbf { x } , t )$ . As t changes, this conditional distribution changes, and so the averaged velocity generally changes as well. Consequently, the marginal ODE trajectory

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d} \mathbf {z} _ {t}}{\mathrm{d} t} = \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {z} _ {t}, t) $$

need not be a straight-line interpolation in time, even though every underlying conditional path is straight. Therefore, while the scheduler $( a _ { t } , b _ { t } ) = ( 1 - t , t )$ can still be useful in practice, any empirical gain should not be attributed solely to a supposed “straightness” of the resulting ODE trajectories. In particular, since the marginal ODE trajectories are not generally straight, this choice alone does not imply that one can use fewer solver steps while still sampling the ODE accurately.

We now illustrate this point with a simple closed-form counterexample.

Example: A Simple 1D Counterexample

Consider the one-dimensional setting with

$$ x _ {0} \sim \mathcal {N} (0, \sigma_ {0} ^ {2}), \qquad x _ {1} \sim \mathcal {N} (0, \sigma_ {1} ^ {2}), $$

independent coupling, and the canonical afine interpolation $x _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) x _ { 0 } + t x _ { 1 }$ Each conditional path $( 1 - t ) x _ { 0 } + t x _ { 1 }$ is a straight line in time. We now compute the induced marginal velocity field.

Since $( x _ { 1 } - x _ { 0 } , x _ { t } )$ is jointly Gaussian, the conditional expectation is linear in x, so

$$ v ^ {*} (x, t) = \mathbb {E} [ x _ {1} - x _ {0} | x _ {t} = x ] = \frac {\operatorname{Cov} (x _ {1} - x _ {0} , x _ {t})}{\operatorname{Var} (x _ {t})} x. $$

A direct calculation gives

$$ \operatorname{Var} (x _ {t}) = (1 - t) ^ {2} \sigma_ {0} ^ {2} + t ^ {2} \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} =: V (t), $$

and

$$ \operatorname{Cov} (x _ {1} - x _ {0}, x _ {t}) = t \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} - (1 - t) \sigma_ {0} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{2} V ^ {\prime} (t). $$

Hence

$$ v ^ {*} (x, t) = \frac {V ^ {\prime} (t)}{2 V (t)} x. $$

The marginal ODE

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d} z _ {t}}{\mathrm{d} t} = v ^ {*} (z _ {t}, t) $$

therefore has the exact solution

$$ z _ {t} = z _ {0} \sqrt {\frac {V (t)}{V (0)}} = z _ {0} \sqrt {(1 - t) ^ {2} + \frac {\sigma_ {1} ^ {2}}{\sigma_ {0} ^ {2}} t ^ {2}}. $$

Now, a straight-line interpolation in time would require $z _ { t }$ to be afine in $t ,$ namely $z _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) z _ { 0 } + t z _ { 1 }$ for some endpoint $z _ { 1 }$ . But

$$ \sqrt {(1 - t) ^ {2} + \frac {\sigma_ {1} ^ {2}}{\sigma_ {0} ^ {2}} t ^ {2}} $$

is not afine in t for any positive $\sigma _ { 0 } , \sigma _ { 1 }$ . Thus the ODE trajectory is generally not a straight-line interpolation in time. We illustrate this phenomenon in Section 5.3.5 with the example $\sigma _ { 0 } = 2$ and $\sigma _ { 1 } = 3$

For example, when $\sigma _ { 0 } = \sigma _ { 1 }$ , we have $z _ { 1 } = z _ { 0 }$ , so a straight-line interpolation would simply be the constant path $z _ { t } ~ = ~ z _ { 0 }$ . In contrast, the actual ODE trajectory satisfies

$$ z _ {1 / 2} = \frac {z _ {0}}{\sqrt {2}} \neq z _ {0}. $$

Therefore, even though every conditional interpolation path is straight, the induced marginal ODE trajectory need not be. ■

Gray lines show sampled conditional paths $( 1 - t ) x _ { 0 } + t x _ { 1 }$ , which are straight by construction. Solid curves show the exact marginal ODE trajectories $z _ { t } = z _ { 0 } \sqrt { ( 1 - t ) ^ { 2 } + ( \sigma _ { 1 } / \sigma _ { 0 } ) ^ { 2 } t ^ { 2 } }$ , and dashed lines show the corresponding straight-line interpolations from $z _ { 0 }$ to z . The visible gap between solid and dashed curves confirms that the marginal ODE trajectories are not afine in $t ,$ even though every conditional path is a straight line. 5.4 (Optional) Properties of the Canonical Afine Flow

Given two endpoint distributions $p _ { 0 } = p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { 1 } = p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ , a natural and widely used choice for defining the conditional path in flow matching (FM) (Lipman et $a l .$ 2022) and rectified flow (RF) (Liu, Gong, et al., 2022) is the linear interpolation

$$ a _ {t} = 1 - t, \qquad b _ {t} = t, $$

which yields the interpolant

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = (1 - t) \mathbf {x} _ {0} + t \mathbf {x} _ {1}, \qquad \mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p _ {\mathrm{src}}, \mathbf {x} _ {1} \sim p _ {\mathrm{tgt}}. $$

Under this choice, the training objective simplifies to

$$ \mathbb {E} _ {t \sim \mathcal {U} [ 0, 1 ]} \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}, \mathbf {x} _ {1}} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - (\mathbf {x} _ {1} - \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]. $$

This linear flow enjoys several appealing properties. In particular, it admits an iterative refinement scheme, known as Reflow, which progressively straightens the path between distributions while preserving the marginals.

5.4.1 Rectifying Flows: From Noisy Guesses to Structured Pairings

From Noise to Data via Coherent Paths. Take the generation task where $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ is the prior and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ is the real data. We want a continuous path that transports noise to data. A naive tactic samples ${ \bf z } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ independently, interpolates $( \mathrm { e . g . ~ } \mathbf { x } _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } + t \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } )$ , and fits a velocity field to that line. This creates incoherent pairings: endpoints are unrelated across iterations, so trajectories fluctuate, variance explodes, convergence slows, and sample quality sufers.

Why Independent Couplings Fall Short. Conditional flow matching with inde pendent draws uses

$$ \pi (\mathbf {z}) = p _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) p _ {\mathrm{tgt}} (\mathbf {x} _ {1}), $$

or one-sided variants. Such couplings are sampling-friendly but induce jagged, high-variance paths that a velocity field struggles to model.

Rectify the Flow via Dependent Coupling. Rather than relying on arbitrary pairings, we use a pre-trained difusion model $\mathbf { v } _ { \phi } \times \left( \cdot , t \right)$ as the drift in a PF-ODE to deterministically transport each source point. Starting from $\mathbf { z } ( 0 ) = \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ we integrate

$$ \frac {\mathrm{d} \mathbf {z} (t)}{\mathrm{d} t} = \mathbf {v} _ {\phi^ {\times}} (\mathbf {z} (t), t), \qquad t \in [ 0, 1 ], $$

to obtain $\hat { \mathbf { z } } _ { 1 } : = \mathbf { z } ( 1 )$ positioned near the data space learned from the pre-trained model. The resulting pair $\left( \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } , \hat { \mathbf { z } } _ { 1 } \right)$ forms a dependent coupling: it follows a structured, model-guided path rather than an arbitrary interpolation. This idea extend naturally to afine reference paths of the form ${ \bf x } _ { t } = a _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 0 } + b _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 1 }$ , where $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $\mathbf { x } _ { 1 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$

Algorithm 2 Rectify Operation

Input: Reference path $\{x_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}$ (e.g. $x_{t}=a_{t}x_{0}+b_{t}x_{1}$ )

1: Pre-Train Diffusion. Fit $v_{\phi^{\times}}$ on the chosen path by minimizing $\phi^{\times}\in\arg\min_{\phi}\mathbb{E}_{t,x_{0},x_{1}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{v}_{\phi}(x_{t},t)-\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]$ .

2: Rectify. Sample $z_{0}\sim p_{src}$ and integrate $\frac{\mathrm{d}z(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\mathbf{v}_{\phi^{\times}}(\mathbf{z}(t),t),\quad\mathbf{z}(0)=\mathbf{z}_{0},\quad t\in[0,1]$ ,

to obtain $\hat{z}_{1}=\mathbf{z}(1)$ and the trajectory $\{z(t)\}_{t\in[0,1]}$ .

Output: Dependent (coherent) pair $(\mathbf{z}_{0},\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{1})$ or the full trajectory.

Why It Works: Marginal-Preserving Structure. Let $\Phi_{0\rightarrow t}$ denote the flow map generated by the above ODE defined by the pre-trained diffusion $v_{\phi^{\times}}$ ; then $\mathbf{z}(t)=\Phi_{0\rightarrow t}(\mathbf{z}_{0})$ and $\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{1}=\Phi_{0\rightarrow1}(\mathbf{z}_{0})$ . The Rectify procedure pairs each source point with its flow endpoint, giving the deterministic joint $\pi_{\text{Rectify}}(\mathbf{z}_{0},\mathbf{z}_{1})=p_{\text{src}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\delta(\mathbf{z}_{1}-\Phi_{0\rightarrow1}(\mathbf{z}_{0}))$ .

We have two immediate consequences:

■ Source Marginal is Preserved: $\int\pi_{\text{Rectify}}(\mathbf{z}_{0},\mathbf{z}_{1})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}_{1}=p_{\text{src}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})$ .

■ Pushforward Along the Flow: $(\Phi_{0\rightarrow t})_{\#}p_{\text{src}}=\text{Law}(\mathbf{z}(t))$ , i.e., the time-t distribution is the pushforward of $p_{src}$ by $\Phi_{0\rightarrow t}$ .

If $v_{\phi^{\times}}$ matches the oracle drift of a given reference path $x_{t}$ , then all intermediate marginals coincide: $\text{Law}(\mathbf{z}(t))=\text{Law}(\mathbf{x}_{t}),\quad\text{for all } t\in[0,1],\quad\text{and}\quad(\Phi_{0\rightarrow1})_{\#}p_{\text{src}}=p_{\text{tgt}}$ .

Summary. Rectification replaces noisy independent pairings with smooth teacher-guided trajectories, lowering variance, easing optimization, and improving samples. The idea covers canonical linear paths $x_{t}=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}$ and general affine forms $x_{t}=a_{t}x_{0}+b_{t}x_{1}$ .

For the canonical path, repeatedly applying Rectify $( ^ { \ast } R e f l o w ^ { \ast } )$ further straightens trajectories without increasing transport cost, making training stil easier.

5.4.2 Reflow: Iteratively Straightening Flows

Why Reflow? Independent pairings often induce irregular and meandering ODE trajectories between $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ , which increase discretization error and variance during simulation. This raises a natural question:

Question 5.4.1

Can we learn couplings that induce transport paths that are closer to straigh lines between the two distributions, while still preserving the correct marginals?

This motivates $_ { R e f l }$ ow : repeatedly apply Rectify to update the coupling so that successive flows become easier to integrate.

Core Idea: Recursive Straightening via Rectify. Start from the canonica interpolation on the product coupling $\pi ^ { ( 0 ) } : = p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ) p _ { \mathrm { t g t } } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 1 } )$

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {t} = (1 - t) \mathbf {x} _ {0} + t \mathbf {x} _ {1}. $$

Applying Rectify replaces the independent pairing with a dependent one $\left( \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } , \hat { \mathbf { z } } _ { 1 } \right)$ which empirically induces lower-curvature trajectories under the learned field. Iterating this update progressively reduces path curvature (never forcing literal straight lines), improving numerical stability and alignment.

The Reflow Procedure. Each iteration performs two steps:

■ Re-Fit Flow: Train a new velocity field from samples of the current coupling:

$$ \phi_ {k + 1} = \arg \min _ {\phi} \mathcal {L} \left(\phi \mid \pi^ {(k)}\right), \quad \text { where } $$

$$ \mathcal {L} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} \Big | \pi^ {(k)}\right) := \mathbb {E} _ {t, (\mathbf {z} _ {0} ^ {(k)}, \hat {\mathbf {z}} _ {1} ^ {(k)}) \sim \pi^ {(k)}} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {z} _ {t}, t) - (\hat {\mathbf {z}} _ {1} ^ {(k)} - \mathbf {z} _ {0} ^ {(k)}) \right\| ^ {2} \right]\tag{5.4.1} $$

with $\mathbf { z } _ { t } = t \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } ^ { ( k ) } + ( 1 - t ) \hat { \mathbf { z } } _ { 1 } ^ { ( k ) }$

■ Generate New Coupling: Solve the learned ODE starting from new source samples $\mathbf { z } _ { 0 } ^ { ( k + 1 ) } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$

$$ \hat {\mathbf {z}} _ {1} ^ {(k + 1)} \leftarrow \mathbf {z} _ {0} ^ {(k + 1)} + \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {\phi_ {k + 1}} (\mathbf {z} (t), t) \mathrm{d} t, $$

and define the updated coupling:

$$ \pi^ {(k + 1)} (\mathbf {z} _ {0}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}) := p _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathbf {z} _ {0}) \delta \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1} - \hat {\mathbf {z}} _ {1} ^ {(k + 1)}\right). $$

In other words, Reflow can be viewed as repeatedly applying the Rectify operator, producing a sequence of progressively refined couplings:

$$ \pi^ {(k + 1)} = \text { Rectify } \left(\pi^ {(k)}\right)\tag{5.4.2} $$

so that both the flow and the coupling evolve together, yielding progressively more stable transport paths.

5.4.3 Properties of Reflow

Two key theoretical properties drive the usefulness of Reflow: it reduces transport cost and it straightens the trajectories.

I. Reflow Never Increases Transport Cost. Let $c ( \mathbf { y } )$ be a convex cost function $( \mathrm { e . g . , \ } \| \mathbf { y } \| _ { 2 } ^ { p }$ with $p \geq 1 )$ . Each Rectify step forms a new coupling $\left( \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } , \hat { \mathbf { z } } _ { 1 } \right)$ whose cost is no worse than the original:

Proposition 5.4.1: Rectify May Reduce Transport Costs

Assuming an ideal velocity field $\mathbf { v } ^ { * } = \mathbf { v } _ { \phi } \times$ , we have:

$$ \mathbb {E} \left[ c (\hat {\mathbf {z}} _ {1} - \mathbf {z} _ {0}) \right] \leq \mathbb {E} \left[ c (\mathbf {x} _ {1} - \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \right]. $$

Proof for Proposition.

Follows from Jensen’s inequality. See Liu, Gong, et al. (2022) for a full derivation.

Applying this result recursively shows that the Reflow process does not increase the transport cost.

II. Reflow Straightens the Path. The longer we iterate Reflow, the straighter the learned trajectories may become. To measure this, define the straightness functional of a path $\mathbf { Y } = \{ \mathbf { y } _ { t } \} _ { t \in [ 0 , 1 ] }$ as

$$ \mathcal {S} (\mathbf {Y}) := \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {y} _ {1} - \mathbf {y} _ {0} - \frac {\mathrm{d} \mathbf {y} _ {t}}{\mathrm{d} t} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] \mathrm{d} t. $$

If $\begin{array} { r } { S ( \mathbf { Y } ) = 0 } \end{array}$ , then Y is exactly a straight line.

Proposition 5.4.2: Reflow Straightens the Stochastic Path

For rectified paths $\mathbf { Z } ^ { ( k ) }$ , we have:

$$ \min _ {k \in \{0, \dots , K \}} \mathcal {S} (\mathbf {Z} ^ {(k)}) \leq \frac {\mathbb {E} \left[ \| \mathbf {x} _ {1} - \mathbf {x} _ {0} \| ^ {2} \right]}{K}. $$

https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-28/f07f65a6-cc54-4879-9a05-fbe8b1db7b7e/e4bf6e884ac6bcd332ef82bd56622dc0ccd5e7fe4c45dcce1146782f4f85e0cf.jpg" alt="image" loading="lazy">

Figure 5.10: Illustration of Reflow. Paths become progressively straighter with Rectify procedure. Source: Adapted from Liu, Gong, et al. (2022).

Proof for Proposition.

See Theorem 3.7 of Liu (2022).

The FM or RF formulation with linear interpolation kernels, together with the Reflow procedure, provides a simpler training objective and a practical method for refining stochastic couplings. For theoretical details, we refer readers to (Liu, Gong, et al., 2022; Liu, 2022).

III. Connection to Optimal Transport. Lastly, we note that straight-line couplings are not necessarily optimal in the sense of optimal transport (OT). This involves some terminology that will be introduced in Section 7.2; we therefore refer readers who are not familiar with OT to that section.

A hallmark of quadratic-cost optimal transport is that particles travel along straight lines: a particle at $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } }$ moves to $\mathbf { T } ( \mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } } )$ via $\mathbf { x } _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } + t \mathbf { T } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } )$ , where T is the optimal transport map. However, not every map S generating such straight line paths, i.e., $\mathbf { x } _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } + t \mathbf { S } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } )$ , is optimal. The map S yields the optima flow only if it minimizes the Monge cost $\mathbb { E } [ \| \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } - \mathbf { S } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ) \| ^ { 2 } ]$ ]. Thus, while straight-line paths are necessary, they are not suficient; optimality also depends on the correct endpoint map T.

Example: Straight Couplings Need Not Be Optima

Let $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } } = p _ { \mathrm { t g t } } = \mathcal { N } ( \mathbf { 0 } , \mathbf { I } )$ . For the cost $c ( \mathbf { x } , \mathbf { y } ) = \| \mathbf { x } - \mathbf { y } \| ^ { p }$ with $p > 0$ , the c-optimal coupling is the identity coupling $\pi ^ { }$ , where $\pi ^ { }$ is the law of $( \mathbf { x } , \mathbf { x } )$ with $\mathbf { x } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$

Now consider the coupling $\pi _ { \mathbf { A } }$ defined as the law of $( \mathbf { x } , \mathbf { A x } )$ , where $\mathbf { x } \sim p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and A is a rotation matrix satisfying $\mathbf { A } ^ { \top } \mathbf { A } = \mathbf { I }$ , det $( \mathbf { A } ) = 1 , \mathbf { A } \neq \mathbf { I }$ , and −1 is not an eigenvalue. Then $\pi _ { \mathbf { A } }$ is a valid coupling of $p _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ and $p _ { \mathrm { t g t } }$ , and corresponds to straight-line paths between x and Ax, but it is not c-optimal for any twice diferentiable strictly convex cost c with invertible Hessian. The suboptimality arises from the rotational transformation. As discussed in Equation (7.5.2), even removing the rotation may not lead to an optimal coupling. ■

We will continue exploring the connection to OT in Section 7.5.2.

5.5 Closing Remarks

This chapter has illuminated the third and final foundational perspective on difusion models, one rooted in the principles of deterministic flows. Our exploration began with Normalizing Flows (NFs), which leverage the change-of-variables formula to learn an exact, invertible mapping between a simple prior and the data distribution. We then saw this concept evolve into a continuous-time process with Neural ODEs, where a learned velocity field governs the transformation. However, this approach comes with the significant drawback of requiring costly ODE simulations within the training loop.

The modern framework of Flow Matching (FM) was presented as an elegant and eficient solution to this challenge. By pre-defining a probability path $\{ p _ { t } \} _ { t }$ and a corresponding velocity field that satisfies the continuity equation, FM establishes a clear target for the ODE flow. Crucially, just as we saw in the variational and score-based views, FM employs a powerful conditioning trick. This transforms the intractable problem of matching the marginal velocity field into a simple and tractable regression against a known conditional velocity, making training entirely simulation-free. This perspective recasts difusion models themselves as a special case of learning a deterministic flow to transport a Gaussian prior to the data distribution.

With the introduction of the flow-based view, our survey of the three conceptual pillars of difusion modeling is now complete. Throughout this journey, a remarkable pattern has emerged: each framework, despite its unique origins in VAEs, EBMs, or NFs, has converged on a continuous-time generative process and has relied on a conditioning strategy to enable tractable learning.

In the next chapter, we will finally synthesize these parallel threads into a single, unified framework. We will:

  1. Formally demonstrate that the variational, score-based, and flow-based perspectives are not merely analogous but are mathematically equivalent at a fundamental level.
  1. Show how the Fokker-Planck equation serves as the universal law governing density evolution across all three views, revealing that they are simply diferent lenses for describing the same core generative principle.

This unified lens will provide a complete and systematic understanding of the modern difusion paradigm.

A Unified and Systematic Lens on Difusion Models

Mathematics is the art of giving the same name to diferent things.

Henri Poincaré

This chapter presents a systematic viewpoint that connects the variational based, score-based, and flow-based perspectives within a coherent picture. While motivated by diferent intuitions, these approaches converge on the same core mechanism underlying modern difusion methods. Building on Chapters 2 to 5, we observe a common recipe: define a forward corruption process that traces a path of marginals, then learn a time varying vector field that transports a simple prior to the data distribution along this path.

A key ingredient across all perspectives is the conditioning trick introduced in Section 6.1, which transforms an intractable marginal objective into a tractable conditional one, leading to stable and eficient training.

In Section 6.2 we analyze the training objective in a systematic way, identifying its essential components and clarifying how loss functions are formulated in the variational, score-based, and flow-based viewpoints.

Section 6.3 shows that any afine forward noise injection of the form $\mathbf { x } _ { t } =$ $\alpha _ { t } { \bf x } _ { 0 } + \sigma _ { t } \epsilon$ can be equivalently transformed into the standard linear schedule ${ \bf x } _ { t } = ( 1 - t ) { \bf x } _ { 0 } + t { \bf \epsilon }$ . Moreover, common parameterizations such as noise prediction, clean data prediction, score prediction, and velocity prediction are interchangeable at the level of gradients. Thus, the choices of noise schedulers and parameterizations both adhere to the same modeling principle.

Finally, Section 6.4 brings the discussion together and identifies the governing rule: the Fokker–Planck equation. Whether viewed as a variational scheme (dis crete time denoising), a score-based method (SDE formulation), or a flow-based method (ODE formulation), each constructs a generator whose marginals follow the same density evolution. The Fokker–Planck equation thus serves as the universal constraint respected by all three viewpoints, with diferences arising only in parameterization and training objectives.

6.1 Conditional Tricks: The Secret Sauce of Difusion Models

Until now, we have explored difusion models from three seemingly distinct origins: variational, score-based, and flow based perspectives. Each was originally motivated by diferent goals and led to its own training objectives (with a fixed t):

■ Variational View: Learn a parametrized density $p _ { \phi } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t - \Delta t } | \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ to approximate the oracle reverse transition $p ( \mathbf { x } _ { t - \Delta t } | \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ by minimizing:

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{KL}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t})} \left[ \mathcal {D} _ {\mathrm{KL}} \big (p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) \| p _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) \big) \right]; $$

■ Score-Based View: Learn a score model $\mathbf { s } _ { \phi } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } , t )$ to approximate the margina score $\nabla _ { \mathbf { x } } \log p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ via:

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{SM}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t})} \left[ \| \mathbf {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]; $$

■ Flow-Based View: Learn a velocity model $\mathbf { v } _ { \phi } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } , t )$ to match the oracle velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } ) \ ( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , defined by Equation (5.2.10)) by minimizing:

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{FM}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t})} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]. $$

At first glance, these objectives seem hopelessly intractable, since they all require access to oracle quantities that are fundamentally unknowable in general. But here comes the exciting twist: each method independently arrives at the same elegant solution to this problem: conditioning on the data $\mathbf { x } _ { \mathrm { 0 } }$ . This technique transforms each intractable training target into a tractable one.

This elegant “conditioning technique” rewrites the objectives as expectations over the known Gaussian conditionals $p _ { t } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } | \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } )$ , yielding gradient-equivalent closed form regression targets and tractable training objectives:

■ Variational View (Equation (2.2.3)):

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{KL}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) = \underbrace {\mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}} \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0})} \left[ \mathcal {D} _ {\mathrm{KL}} \big (p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} , \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \| p _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) \big) \right]} _ {\mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{CKL}} (\boldsymbol {\phi})} + C; $$

■ Score-Based View (Equation (3.3.3)):

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{SM}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) = \underbrace {\mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}} \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0})} \left[ \| \mathbf {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} , t) - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]} _ {\mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{DSM}} (\boldsymbol {\phi})} + C; $$

■ Flow-Based View (Equation (5.2.9)):

$$ \mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{FM}} (\phi) = \underbrace {\mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}} \mathbb {E} _ {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0})} \left[ \| \mathbf {v} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} , t) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \| ^ {2} \right]} _ {\mathcal {J} _ {\mathrm{CFM}} (\phi)} + C. $$

To build a unified view, we next revisit the conditional KL, score, and velocity objectives in a systematic manner. Crucially, these objectives are not only tractable but also equivalent to their original forms up to a constant vertical shift. The conditional versions $( \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { C K L } } , \mathcal { T } _ { \mathrm { D S M } } , \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { C F M } } )$ difer from the originals $( \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { K L } } , ~ \mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { S M } }$ $\mathcal { I } _ { \mathrm { F M } } )$ only by this shift, which leaves the gradients unchanged and thus preserve the optimization landscape. As a result, the minimizers remain uniquely identified with the true oracle targets, since each reduces to a least-squares regression problem whose solution recovers the corresponding conditional expectation:

$$ \begin{array}{r l} p ^ {} (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t})} [ p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}, \mathbf {x} _ {0}) ] & = p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}), \\ \mathbf {s} ^ {} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t})} [ \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) ] & = \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}), \\ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t})} [ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) ] & = \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}). \end{array}\tag{6.1.1} $$

Example: Closed-Form Oracle Targets on a Finite Dataset

To make Equation (6.1.1) more concrete, suppose the data distribution is the empirical measure

$$ \hat {p} _ {0} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \delta_ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}}, $$

where $\{ \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ^ { ( i ) } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ denotes the finite training set.

In Equation (6.1.1), the notation

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) $$

means that we average over the posterior distribution of the clean sample given the noisy observation $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ . Equivalently, for any quantity $\mathbf { h } ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } )$ ，

$$ \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t})} [ \mathbf {h} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) ] = \int \mathbf {h} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) p (\mathbf {x} _ {0} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) \mathrm{d} \mathbf {x} _ {0}. $$

By Bayes’ rule,

$$ p (\mathbf {x} _ {0} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \frac {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) p _ {\mathrm{data}} (\mathbf {x} _ {0})}{\int p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {0}) p _ {\mathrm{data}} (\tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {0}) \mathrm{d} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {0}}. $$

Under the standard Gaussian difusion forward process,

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}) = \mathcal {N} \left(\mathbf {x} _ {t}; \alpha_ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}, \sigma_ {t} ^ {2} \mathbf {I}\right), $$

so the conditional likelihood is explicit. However, at the population level, the posterior $p ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } | \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ still requires averaging over the full data distribution $p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } } .$

If we now replace $p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ by the empirical measure

$$ \hat {p} _ {0} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \delta_ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}}, $$

then the posterior $p ( \mathbf { x } _ { 0 } | \mathbf { x } _ { t } )$ is supported only on the training examples, and its mass at $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } ^ { ( i ) }$ is

$$ p (\mathbf {x} _ {0} = \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \frac {p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)})}{\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N} p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(j)})} =: w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t), \qquad \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = 1. $$

Hence the posterior expectation above reduces to the finite sum

$$ \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0} \sim p (\cdot | \mathbf {x} _ {t})} [ \mathbf {h} (\mathbf {x} _ {0}) ] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \mathbf {h} (\mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}). $$

Thus, in the finite-dataset case, the abstract posterior averaging in Equa tion (6.1.1) becomes a concrete weighted average over the training set, with weights $w _ { i } ( \mathbf { x } _ { t } , t )$ . We now make these three cases explicit.

Variational View. The oracle reverse transition becomes

$$ p ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}, \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}). $$

Here each conditional reverse kernel

$$ p (\mathbf {x} _ {t - \Delta t} | \mathbf {x} _ {t}, \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}) $$

is itself available in closed form under Gaussian difusion; in fact, it is Gaussian as shown in Lemma 2.2.2. So the true reverse kernel is simply a posterior-weighted mixture of explicit Gaussian bridges.

Score-Based View. Likewise, the oracle score becomes

$$ \mathbf {s} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}). $$

The conditional score is explicit:

$$ \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}) = - \frac {\mathbf {x} _ {t} - \alpha_ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}}{\sigma_ {t} ^ {2}}. $$

Therefore,

$$ \mathbf {s} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = - \frac {1}{\sigma_ {t} ^ {2}} \left(\mathbf {x} _ {t} - \alpha_ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}\right). $$

So the marginal score points from the noisy sample toward a posterior-weighted average of training examples.

Flow-Based View. The same phenomenon appears for flow matching:

$$ \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}). $$

Again, the building blocks are explicit. The corresponding conditional velocity field has closed form:

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)}) = \alpha_ {t} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)} + \frac {\sigma_ {t} ^ {\prime}}{\sigma_ {t}} \big (\mathbf {x} _ {t} - \alpha_ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {(i)} \big). $$

Hence the oracle velocity is also an explicit posterior-weighted average over the dataset.

Taken together, these formulas show that, on a finite dataset, all three oracle targets reduce to explicit posterior-weighted averages of closed-form conditional quantities. ■

The common conditional reformulation is no coincidence: by making training tractable, it reveals a profound unification. Variational difusion, score-based SDEs, and flow matching are simply diferent facets of the same principle. Three perspectives, one insight, elegantly connected. We will continue to explore their equivalence throughout the rest of this chapter.

6.2 A Roadmap for Elucidating Training Losses in Difusion Models

This section builds a systematic view of training losses in difusion models. In Section 6.2.1, we extend the standard three objectives to a broader set of four parameterizations, showing how they arise from diferent modeling perspectives. In Section 6.2.2, we then distill these results into a general framework that disentangle the structure of difusion objectives, laying the groundwork for the equivalence results in Section 6.3.

6.2.1 Four Common Parameterizations in Difusion Models

Throughout this section, we consider the forward perturbation kerne

$$ p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) = \mathcal {N} \left(\mathbf {x} _ {t}; \alpha_ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {0}, \sigma_ {t} ^ {2} \mathbf {I}\right), $$

where $\mathbf { x } _ { 0 } \sim p _ { \mathrm { d a t a } }$ , as defined in Equation (4.5.1), unless stated otherwise.

Let $\omega : [ 0 , T ] \mathbb { R } _ { > 0 }$ denote a positive time-weighting function. The four standard parameterizations (noise $\epsilon _ { \phi }$ , clean $\mathbf { x } _ { \phi }$ , score ${ \bf s } _ { \phi }$ , and velocity $\mathbf { v } _ { \phi } )$ , together with their respective minimizers $\epsilon ^ { } , \mathbf { x } ^ { } , \mathbf { s } ^ { }$ , and $\mathbf { v } ^ { }$ , are summarized below for clarity and to facilitate further discussion.

Variational View. Based on the KL divergence in DDPMs (see Sections 2.2.4 and 4.5.3), this approach reduces to predicting either the expected noise that produces $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ or the expected clean signal that $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ was perturbed from.

  1. ϵ-Prediction (Noise Prediction) (Ho et al., 2020):

$$ \epsilon_ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \approx \mathbb {E} [ \epsilon | \mathbf {x} _ {t} ] = \epsilon^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t)\tag{6.2.1} $$

with training objective

$$ \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{noise}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {t} \left[ \omega (t) \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\epsilon}} \left\| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \boldsymbol {\epsilon} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]. $$

Here, $\epsilon ^ { * }$ means the average noise that was injected to obtain the given $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$

  1. x-Prediction (Clean Prediction) (Kingma et al., 2021; Karras et $a l .$ , 2022; Song et al., 2023):

$$ \mathbf {x} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \approx \mathbb {E} [ \mathbf {x} _ {0} | \mathbf {x} _ {t} ] = \mathbf {x} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t)\tag{6.2.2} $$

with training objective

$$ \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{clean}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {t} \left[ \omega (t) \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}, \epsilon} \left\| \mathbf {x} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \mathbf {x} _ {0} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]. $$

Here, $\mathbf { x } ^ { * }$ means the average of all plausible clean guesses, given the noisy observation $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ .

Score-Based View. Predicts the score function at noise level t, which points in the average direction to denoise $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ back toward all possible clean samples that could have generated it:

  1. Score Prediction (Song and Ermon, 2019; Song et al., 2020c):

$$ \mathbf {s} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \approx \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}) = \mathbb {E} \left[ \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) | \mathbf {x} _ {t} \right] = \mathbf {s} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t)\tag{6.2.3} $$

with training objective

$$ \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{score}} (\boldsymbol {\phi}) := \mathbb {E} _ {t} \left[ \omega (t) \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {x} _ {0}, \epsilon} \left\| \mathbf {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\phi}} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) - \nabla_ {\mathbf {x} _ {t}} \log p _ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} | \mathbf {x} _ {0}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \right], $$

where the conditional score satisfies $\nabla _ { \mathbf { x } _ { t } }$ log $\begin{array} { r } { p _ { t } ( \mathbf x _ { t } | \mathbf x _ { 0 } ) = - \frac { 1 } { \sigma _ { t } } \epsilon . } \end{array}$

Flow-Based View. Predicts the instantaneous average velocity of the data as it evolves through $\mathbf { x } _ { t }$ :

  1. v-Prediction (Velocity Prediction) (Lipman et al., 2022; Liu, 2022; Salimans and Ho, 2021; Albergo et al., 2023):

$$ \mathbf {v} _ {\phi} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t) \approx \mathbb {E} \left[ \frac {\mathrm{d} \mathbf {x} _ {t}}{\mathrm{d} t} \bigg | \mathbf {x} _ {t} \right] = \mathbf {v} ^ {*} (\mathbf {x} _ {t}, t)\tag{6.2.4} $$

with training objective
